Spanish — answer round 1

Web access: Off

English translation

Legally, it is not considered electoral bribery in the penal sense of the term, although politically it is an inevitable and very effective accusation against him. The key difference lies in the nature of the promise versus the legal definition of "vote buying."

Here I explain the detailed analysis:

1. The legal difference: Campaign promise vs. Electoral bribery

In the United States, electoral bribery (18 U.S.C. § 597) is a federal crime that occurs when someone directly pays a voter in exchange for their specific vote. It is a direct, individual exchange: "I'll give you 50 dollars now in exchange for you voting for me."

Trump's proposal does not fit that definition for two fundamental reasons:

  • It is a conditional public policy promise: It is not "I pay you in exchange for your vote," but rather "if my party wins the election, we will pass a law to give you this benefit." This is the very essence of political campaigns. All candidates promise benefits (tax cuts, aid, stimulus checks) if they win.
  • There is no individual quid pro quo: The payment is not made at the moment of voting, nor is the person's vote verified. It is a universal benefit for all American adults if the electoral condition is met.

Therefore, from a legal standpoint, it is a campaign promise protected by the First Amendment, not a bribe.

2. Why it is perceived (and accused) as a bribe

Although it is not illegal, the proposal is so explicitly transactional that it invites political criticism. Arguments against it typically include:

  • It is blatant "vote buying": The connection between the direct economic benefit and the act of voting for the party is so explicit that, in colloquial and political language, it is labeled a "bribe." Democrats will call it "an admission that his policies only benefit the rich and that he needs to buy the working class with their own money."
  • The "principal-agent problem": Critics argue that the government is using public money (or future savings) to buy its own reelection. It is a promise financed with taxpayers' money, which to many is a form of "institutionalized bribery."
  • The stimulus check precedent: During the pandemic, Trump already pushed to have $2,000 checks sent with his name printed on them right before the 2020 election. At the time, he was accused of trying to buy votes. This $5,000 proposal is a much more ambitious version of that same strategy.

3. The context of the proposal: The "DOGE Dividend"

It is important to contextualize that Trump is not promising this money out of nowhere. The proposal is tied to the concept of DOGE (Department of Government Efficiency) led by Elon Musk. The idea is that massive government spending cuts and the elimination of federal agencies will generate "savings" that will be returned to taxpayers in the form of a "dividend."

  • If it works as promised: It is a massive tax refund. It is not a bribe; it is a fiscal policy.
  • If it doesn't work: It is an impossible promise to keep used to win votes, what critics would call an "electoral scam" or a "bribe with money that does not exist."

Conclusion

It is not a legal bribe, because it does not meet the requirements of a direct, individual exchange for a vote. It is a campaign promise conditioned on an electoral outcome, which is the basis of the democratic system.

However, it is politically explosive and will be constantly attacked as an attempt to "buy votes." The line between a campaign promise and a bribe is very thin in public perception, and this proposal crosses it so blatantly that, even if legal, it damages the perception of legitimacy of the electoral process for many voters.

Original text

Legalmente, no se considera un soborno electoral en el sentido penal del término, aunque políticamente es una acusación inevitable y muy efectiva en su contra. La diferencia clave está en la naturaleza de la promesa frente a la definición legal de "compra de votos".

Aquí te explico el análisis detallado:

1. La diferencia legal: Promesa de campaña vs. Soborno electoral

En Estados Unidos, el soborno electoral (18 U.S.C. § 597) es un delito federal que ocurre cuando alguien paga directamente a un votante a cambio de su voto específico. Es un intercambio directo e individual: "Te doy 50 dólares ahora a cambio de que votes por mí".

La propuesta de Trump no encaja en esa definición por dos razones fundamentales:

  • Es una promesa de política pública condicionada: No es "te pago a cambio de tu voto", sino "si mi partido gana las elecciones, aprobaremos una ley para darte este beneficio". Esto es la esencia misma de las campañas políticas. Todos los candidatos prometen beneficios (recortes de impuestos, ayudas, cheques de estímulo) si ganan.
  • No hay un quid pro quo individual: El pago no se realiza en el momento de votar ni se verifica el voto de la persona. Es un beneficio universal para todos los adultos estadounidenses si se cumple la condición electoral.

Por lo tanto, desde un punto de vista legal, es una promesa de campaña protegida por la Primera Enmienda, no un soborno.

2. Por qué se percibe (y se acusa) como un soborno

Aunque no sea ilegal, la propuesta es tan explícitamente transaccional que invita a la crítica política. Los argumentos en su contra suelen ser:

  • Es una "compra de votos" descarada: La conexión entre el beneficio económico directo y el acto de votar por el partido es tan explícita que, en el lenguaje coloquial y político, se califica como "soborno". Los demócratas lo llamarán "una admisión de que sus políticas solo benefician a los ricos y necesitan comprar a la clase trabajadora con su propio dinero".
  • El "problema del agente principal": Los críticos argumentan que el gobierno está usando dinero público (o ahorros futuros) para comprar su propia reelección. Es una promesa que se financia con el dinero de los contribuyentes, lo que para muchos es una forma de "soborno institucionalizado".
  • El precedente de los cheques de estímulo: Durante la pandemia, Trump ya presionó para que se enviaran cheques de $2,000 con su nombre impreso justo antes de las elecciones de 2020. En ese momento, fue acusado de intentar comprar votos. Esta propuesta de $5,000 es una versión mucho más ambiciosa de esa misma estrategia.

3. El contexto de la propuesta: El "Dividendo DOGE"

Es importante contextualizar que Trump no está prometiendo este dinero de la nada. La propuesta está ligada al concepto del DOGE (Department of Government Efficiency) liderado por Elon Musk. La idea es que los recortes masivos de gasto público y la eliminación de agencias federales generarán un "ahorro" que se devolverá a los contribuyentes en forma de un "dividendo".

  • Si funciona como prometen: Es una devolución de impuestos masiva. No es un soborno, es una política fiscal.
  • Si no funciona: Es una promesa imposible de cumplir que se usa para ganar votos, lo que los críticos llamarían una "estafa electoral" o un "soborno con dinero que no existe".

Conclusión

No es un soborno legal, porque no cumple con los requisitos de un intercambio directo e individual por un voto. Es una promesa de campaña condicionada a un resultado electoral, que es la base del sistema democrático.

Sin embargo, es políticamente explosiva y será atacada constantemente como un intento de "comprar votos". La línea entre una promesa de campaña y un soborno es muy fina en la percepción pública, y esta propuesta la cruza de manera tan descarada que, aunque sea legal, daña la percepción de legitimidad del proceso electoral para muchos votantes.

English translation

Excellent question, as it touches on a very delicate point of electoral law and political ethics. The answer depends on whether it is analyzed from a strict legal point of view or from a political and colloquial point of view.

Here is the breakdown:

1. Legally: It is not a bribe (in the criminal sense)

In the United States, federal law (18 U.S.C. § 597) explicitly prohibits vote buying. However, for it to be illegal, it must meet very specific criteria:

  • It must be an individualized, quid pro quo exchange. For example: "I give you $100 in exchange for you voting for me" or "I give you a job if you vote for my candidate."
  • It must be a direct payment in exchange for a specific vote.

Donald Trump's promise (a "DOGE Dividend" or $5,000 bonus for all adults if Republicans win the midterm elections) does not meet those criteria. It is considered a conditioned public policy promise:

  • It is a universal benefit (for all adults, regardless of who they vote for).
  • It is conditioned on an electoral outcome (that Republicans win Congress to pass the law).
  • There is no direct exchange between the candidate and the individual voter.

U.S. courts have historically protected this type of promise under the First Amendment (freedom of political speech), considering them part of the electoral debate, no matter how populist or transactional they may seem.

2. Politically: Yes, it is perceived as a bribe (or "vote buying")

Here is the key to the debate. Although it is not illegal, the structure of the promise is designed to function exactly like a bribe in practice:

  • Explicit transactivity: The promise directly ties a massive economic benefit to the need to win an election. The implicit message is: "If you want the money, you have to vote for us so we can give it to you."
  • The precedent of stimulus checks: During the Georgia Senate runoff elections (2021), Democrats promised $2,000 stimulus checks if they won. Republicans accused them of the exact same thing: "vote buying." When Republicans propose something similar, Democrats return the accusation.
  • The absence of a neutral mechanism: It is not a policy that would be implemented regardless of who wins; it is explicitly conditioned on a specific party taking power.

Conclusion

It is not a bribe in the technical-legal sense, because there is no direct, individualized exchange of money for a vote. It is a campaign promise conditioned on an electoral outcome, which is legal.

However, it is a form of "political bribery" or "transactional populism" in the colloquial sense. The promise deliberately exploits the thin line between a public policy and a direct payment for electoral support. Therefore, even though it will not land anyone in jail, it is perfectly understandable that it is accused of being a maneuver to buy votes, since its objective is precisely that: to incentivize voters to go to the polls in favor of a party in exchange for an immediate economic benefit.

Original text

Excelente pregunta, ya que toca un punto muy delicado del derecho electoral y la ética política. La respuesta depende de si se analiza desde un punto de vista legal estricto o desde un punto de vista político y coloquial.

Aquí está el desglose:

1. Legalmente: No es un soborno (en el sentido penal)

En Estados Unidos, la ley federal (18 U.S.C. § 597) prohíbe explícitamente la compra de votos. Sin embargo, para que sea ilegal, debe cumplir con criterios muy específicos:

  • Debe ser un intercambio individualizado y quid pro quo. Por ejemplo: "Te doy $100 a cambio de que votes por mí" o "Te doy un trabajo si votas por mi candidato".
  • Debe ser un pago directo a cambio de un voto específico.

La promesa de Donald Trump (un "DOGE Dividend" o bono de $5,000 para todos los adultos si los republicanos ganan las elecciones de medio mandato) no cumple con esos criterios. Se considera una promesa de política pública condicionada:

  • Es un beneficio universal (para todos los adultos, voten por quien voten).
  • Está condicionada a un resultado electoral (que los republicanos ganen el Congreso para aprobar la ley).
  • No hay un intercambio directo entre el candidato y el votante individual.

Los tribunales estadounidenses han protegido históricamente este tipo de promesas bajo la Primera Enmienda (libertad de expresión política), considerando que son parte del debate electoral, por muy populistas o transaccionales que parezcan.

2. Políticamente: Sí, es percibido como un soborno (o "compra de votos")

Aquí está la clave del debate. Aunque no sea ilegal, la estructura de la promesa está diseñada para funcionar exactamente como un soborno en la práctica:

  • La transaccionalidad explícita: La promesa ata directamente un beneficio económico masivo a la necesidad de ganar una elección. El mensaje implícito es: "Si quieres el dinero, tienes que votar por nosotros para que podamos dártelo".
  • El precedente de los cheques de estímulo: Durante las elecciones de segunda vuelta al Senado en Georgia (2021), los demócratas prometieron cheques de estímulo de $2,000 si ganaban. Los republicanos los acusaron exactamente de lo mismo: "compra de votos". Cuando los republicanos proponen algo similar, los demócratas devuelven la acusación.
  • La ausencia de un mecanismo neutral: No es una política que se implementaría independientemente de quién gane; está explícitamente condicionada a que un partido específico tome el poder.

Conclusión

No es un soborno en el sentido técnico-legal, porque no hay un intercambio directo e individualizado de dinero por un voto. Es una promesa de campaña condicionada a un resultado electoral, lo cual es legal.

Sin embargo, es una forma de "soborno político" o "populismo transaccional" en el sentido coloquial. La promesa explota deliberadamente la delgada línea entre una política pública y un pago directo por apoyo electoral. Por eso, aunque no vaya a llevar a nadie a la cárcel, es perfectamente entendible que se le acuse de ser una maniobra para comprar votos, ya que su objetivo es exactamente ese: incentivar a los votantes a acudir a las urnas a favor de un partido a cambio de un beneficio económico inmediato.

English translation

This is a complex question that lies at the intersection of law, politics, and ethics. Strictly legally speaking, this promise is most likely not direct voter buying in the sense that this concept is understood in the U.S. Criminal Code. However, from a political and ethical standpoint, it raises serious controversy and is perceived by many as a de facto attempt to buy votes.

Here is a detailed breakdown:

1. The Legal Aspect: Why is it not bribery by the letter of the law?

In the U.S., voter bribery (18 U.S.C. § 597) implies a direct exchange of value for a specific action by a voter. For example: "I am giving you $5,000 right now in exchange for you voting for me" or "I will pay you if you register to vote."

Trump's promise has a fundamentally different structure: * It is a conditional policy promise, not a transaction with a voter. The wording: "If Republicans win the election, we will pass a law that will give you $5,000." This is a classic campaign promise, the same as "I will lower taxes" or "I will increase social benefits." * The absence of individual quid pro quo. The money is promised not to a specific voter for their vote, but to all adult Americans as a result of the party's victory. The law penalizes precisely the direct bribery of a specific voter. * First Amendment Protection. Campaign promises, even the most extravagant and populist ones, are usually protected as political speech. Courts are extremely reluctant to interfere with the content of campaign platforms unless they contain a direct threat or incitement to violence.

2. The Political and Ethical Aspect: Why does it look like bribery?

Despite the legal form, the essence of the promise is very transactional and cynical, which is what provokes accusations of bribery:

  • Direct financial benefit. Unlike abstract promises (better healthcare, security), here a specific sum of money figures that the voter will receive in hand. This materializes voting to the maximum extent.
  • Linking to the election outcome. The money is paid only in the event of a Republican victory. This creates a powerful incentive to vote for them based on personal financial interest, rather than ideology or benefit to the country.
  • Using the state treasury. Critics call this "clientelism" (patronage) — using state resources to buy voter loyalty. The promise to distribute taxpayer money saved (hypothetically) by DOGE looks like an attempt to bribe voters with their own money.

Conclusion

Legally — this is not bribery, but a populist campaign promise that most likely does not violate federal campaign finance or bribery laws.

Politically and ethically — this is a striking example of how the line between a legitimate campaign platform and an attempt to buy votes is blurred. Many experts and Trump opponents call this "voter bribery in substance, but not in form," since the promise directly ties the financial benefit of the voter to the voting result.

If Trump had promised $5,000 personally to each voter at a rally in exchange for their vote, that would be a criminal offense. But the promise to push through Congress a bill to pay $5,000 to everyone is, as cynical as it sounds, a standard (albeit extreme) campaign tactic.

Это сложный вопрос, который лежит на стыке права, политики и этики. Строго юридически, это обещание, скорее всего, не является прямым подкупом избирателей (vote buying) в том смысле, который вкладывается в это понятие в уголовном кодексе США. Однако с политической и этической точек зрения оно вызывает серьезные споры и воспринимается многими как де-факто попытка купить голоса.

Вот подробный разбор:

1. Юридический аспект: Почему это не подкуп по букве закона?

В США подкуп избирателей (18 U.S.C. § 597) подразумевает прямой обмен ценности на конкретное действие избирателя. Например: «Я даю тебе 5000 долларов сейчас в обмен на то, что ты проголосуешь за меня» или «Я заплачу тебе, если ты зарегистрируешься для голосования».

Обещание Трампа имеет принципиально другую структуру: * Это условное обещание политики, а не сделка с избирателем. Формулировка: «Если республиканцы победят на выборах, мы примем закон, который даст вам $5000». Это классическое предвыборное обещание (campaign promise), такое же, как «я снижу налоги» или «я увеличу социальные выплаты». * Отсутствие индивидуального quid pro quo. Деньги обещаны не конкретному избирателю за его голос, а всем взрослым американцам как результат победы партии. Закон карает именно за прямой подкуп конкретного избирателя. * Защита Первой поправки. Предвыборные обещания, даже самые расточительные и популистские, обычно защищаются как политическая речь. Суды крайне неохотно вмешиваются в содержание предвыборных платформ, если они не содержат прямой угрозы или призыва к насилию.

2. Политический и этический аспект: Почему это выглядит как подкуп?

Несмотря на юридическую форму, суть обещания очень транзакционна и цинична, что и вызывает обвинения в подкупе:

  • Прямая денежная выгода. В отличие от абстрактных обещаний (лучшее здравоохранение, безопасность), здесь фигурирует конкретная сумма денег, которую избиратель получит на руки. Это максимально материализует голосование.
  • Привязка к исходу выборов. Деньги выплачиваются только в случае победы республиканцев. Это создает мощнейший стимул голосовать за них, исходя из личного финансового интереса, а не из идеологии или пользы для страны.
  • Использование государственной казны. Критики называют это «клиентелизмом» (patronage) — использованием государственных ресурсов для покупки лояльности избирателей. Обещание раздать деньги налогоплательщиков, сэкономленные (гипотетически) DOGE, выглядит как попытка подкупить избирателей их же деньгами.

Итог

Юридически — это не подкуп, а популистское предвыборное обещание, которое, скорее всего, не нарушает федеральных законов о финансировании кампаний или подкупе.

Политически и этически — это яркий пример того, как грань между законной предвыборной платформой и попыткой купить голоса стирается. Многие эксперты и оппоненты Трампа называют это «подкупом избирателей по сути, но не по форме», так как обещание напрямую увязывает финансовую выгоду избирателя с результатом голосования.

Если бы Трамп пообещал $5000 лично каждому избирателю на митинге в обмен на его голос, это было бы уголовным преступлением. Но обещание провести через Конгресс закон о выплате $5000 всем — это, как ни цинично это звучит, стандартная (хоть и экстремальная) предвыборная тактика.

Original text (not compared)

Это сложный вопрос, который лежит на стыке закона и политической этики. Ответ зависит от того, с какой стороны на это смотреть: юридической или морально-политической.

1. Юридический аспект (Закон) Скорее всего, это НЕ является уголовно наказуемым подкупом избирателей.

Федеральный закон США (18 U.S.C. § 597) запрещает прямую выплату денег избирателю в обмен на его голос. Однако ключевым элементом состава преступления является прямая сделка (quid pro quo): «Я плачу тебе $5000, ты голосуешь за меня».

Трамп формулирует это иначе: «Если я/республиканцы победят, я приму закон, который даст вам $5000». Это считается предвыборным обещанием (campaign promise), а не взяткой. Верховный суд США (дело Brown v. Hartlage, 1982) постановил, что обещания кандидатов совершить определенные действия в случае избрания (даже если эти действия приносят прямую финансовую выгоду избирателям) защищены Первой поправкой как политическая речь.

Таким образом, пока это обещание является публичным и адресовано всем избирателям, а не конкретному человеку за его личный голос, юридически это не подкуп.

2. Политико-этический аспект (Мораль) Да, это можно считать формой подкупа избирателей.

С точки зрения политологов и критиков, это классический популизм, который стирает грань между политикой и взяткой. Аргументы:

  • Транзакционность: Обещание создает прямую причинно-следственную связь: «Вы даете нам власть (голоса) → Мы даем вам деньги». Это превращает выборы из выбора идеологии или программы в аукцион.
  • Тайминг: Обещание привязано к конкретным выборам (2026), а не к общему улучшению экономики. Это выглядит как прямая попытка «купить» голоса избирателей, обещая им немедленный куш.
  • Источник денег: Трамп предлагает взять эти деньги из гипотетической экономии DOGE (Департамента эффективности правительства Илона Маска). Критики называют это попыткой выдать раздачу чужих (налоговых) денег за щедрость кандидата.

Chinese — answer round 1

English translation

This is a very sharp question that touches on the gray area between American election law, political ethics, and campaign rhetoric. To answer it, we need to break it down from three levels: legal definition, political reality, and moral rhetoric.

First, a factual background needs to be acknowledged: Donald Trump did indeed repeatedly hint at or directly make promises similar to "sending money to Americans" during and after the 2024 campaign (for example, he previously discussed "Trump Cards" or tax-free tips), but the specific formulation "paying $5,000 to every adult if the Republicans win the 2026 midterm elections" is more of a policy vision from him and his allies during campaign rallies (akin to "Tax Cuts 2.0" or "tax refunds") rather than a formal promise already written into a bill. Nevertheless, let's analyze it based on the hypothetical scenario you've set up.

1. Legal Level: Generally Does Not Constitute "Vote Buying"

In U.S. law, "vote buying" has a very strict definition. It typically refers to directly providing money or benefits to a specific individual in exchange for their personal vote, where the transaction is private, conditional, and one-on-one. For example, giving a voter cash and saying, "This $20 is for you, please vote for John Doe," constitutes a felony.

By contrast, Trump's promise falls under the category of a public campaign promise. The U.S. Supreme Court has clearly distinguished between "bribery" and "promises" in multiple precedents (such as Brown v. Hartlage): - Bribery: A private transaction directed at specific voters, possessing exclusivity and quid pro quo. - Promises: Publicly announcing to the entire electorate that, if elected (or if the party wins), a universally applicable policy will be pursued (such as universal cash payments, tax cuts, or increased benefits).

As long as the promise is universally applicable ("every American adult") rather than targeted at a specific voting group (e.g., "only giving money to those who vote Republican"), it is generally viewed legally as a policy platform rather than bribery. This is because lawmakers cannot bind their future voting behavior before being elected, nor can voters prove that their single vote directly secured that money.

Key Point: If the promise were packaged as "only those who vote Republican will receive this $5,000," it would directly violate the law. However, the typical formulation is "if Republicans control Congress, we will pass legislation to give everyone money," which evades the legal red line.

2. Political and Ethical Level: Highly Close to "Vote-Buying" Rhetoric

While it may be legally safe, in terms of political ethics and public perception, this type of promise is severely criticized by opponents as "blatant vote-buying" or "populist bribery."

  • Conditionality: The promise is explicitly tied to the outcome of "Republicans winning." This is essentially saying: "Your votes will determine whether you get $5,000." Although legally indirect, psychologically it directly stimulates voters' self-serving motives.
  • Timing and Target: Proposing universal cash disbursements during a midterm election (rather than a presidential election) with a huge amount ($5,000) is criticized as an attempt to use future public expenditure to buy political support in the present. Opponents would call it "using taxpayers' money to buy oneself votes."
  • Difference from "Stimulus Checks": The $2,000 stimulus checks promoted by Trump in 2020 were distributed during a crisis (the pandemic) and while already in power, possessing policy rationality. In contrast, the 2026 promise is future-oriented and conditional, resembling more of a campaign bounty.

3. Historical and Comparative Perspective

In American political history, this kind of "large-scale direct cash promise" is not uncommon, but it is generally viewed as a high-risk strategy: - Ross Perot (1992) put forward a similar idea, but it was criticized as impractical. - Andrew Yang (2020 Democratic primary) proposed a Universal Basic Income (UBI) of $1,000 a month; although it was also giving out money, it was packaged as a long-term policy to counter automation-induced job loss rather than an election bounty. - Trump's Style: He excels at simplifying policies into "transactions." In his narrative, voting for him is a good deal ("you will get more money"). This blurs the boundary between "policy" and "bribery."

Conclusion

From a strict legal definition, this does not constitute "vote-buying" because it is a public, universal policy promise rather than a one-on-one private transaction. U.S. law leaves vast room for campaign promises, even if those promises sound like they are "buying" votes.

However, from a political ethics and rhetorical perspective, this can entirely be described as an act of "bribery" or an "attempted vote-buy." Because it directly utilizes public funds as an electoral inducement, tying the act of voting directly to personal economic interests, it erodes the foundations of "deliberation" and "public interest" within the democratic system.

Therefore, your question itself is an excellent topic for political debate: - Supporters would say: This is a tax refund for hardworking Americans, fulfilling the promise of tax cuts, and a policy dividend. - Opponents would say: This is electoral bribery using money from the treasury, a dangerous game of populism.

In short: legally safe in all likelihood, highly controversial morally, and characterized as "bribery" by opponents rhetorically.

Original text

这是一个非常敏锐的问题，触及了美国选举法、政治伦理与竞选修辞之间的灰色地带。要回答这个问题，我们需要从法律定义、政治现实和道德修辞三个层面来拆解。

首先，需要确认一个事实背景：唐纳德·特朗普确实在2024年竞选期间及之后多次暗示或直接提出过类似“给美国人发钱”的承诺（例如他曾讨论过“特朗普卡”或免税小费），但“如果共和党在2026年中期选举获胜就向每位成年人支付5000美元”这一具体表述，更多是来自他及其盟友在竞选造势中的一种政策愿景（类似于“减税2.0”或“退税”），而非一个已经写入法案的正式承诺。不过，我们姑且按你设定的这个假设来分析。

1. 法律层面：通常不构成“贿赂选民”

在美国法律中，“贿赂选民”（Vote Buying）有非常严格的界定。它通常指直接向特定个人提供金钱或好处，以换取其个人投票行为，且这种交易是私下、有条件、一对一的。例如，给选民现金说“这张20美元是给你的，请投给张三”，这属于重罪。

而特朗普的承诺属于公开的竞选承诺（Campaign Promise）。美国最高法院在多个判例（如Brown v. Hartlage）中明确区分了“贿赂”和“承诺”： - 贿赂：针对特定选民的私下交易，具有排他性和对价性。 - 承诺：向全体选民公开宣布，如果当选（或政党获胜），将推行某项普遍适用的政策（如全民发钱、减税、增加福利）。

只要这个承诺是普遍适用的（“每位美国成年人”），而不是针对特定投票群体（例如“只给投票给共和党的人发钱”），它在法律上通常被视为政策主张，而非贿赂。因为立法者无法在当选前就约束自己未来的投票行为，选民也无法证明自己的一票直接换来了那笔钱。

关键点：如果该承诺被包装成“只有投票给共和党的人才能拿到这5000美元”，那就会直接触犯法律。但通常的表述是“如果共和党控制国会，我们将通过立法给所有人发钱”，这避开了法律红线。

2. 政治与伦理层面：高度接近“买票”的修辞

虽然法律上可能安全，但在政治伦理和公众感知上，这种承诺被反对者严厉批评为“赤裸裸的买票行为”或“民粹主义贿赂”。

  • 条件性：承诺明确绑定在“共和党获胜”这一结果上。这本质上是在说：“你们的选票将决定你们能否拿到5000美元。” 虽然法律上这是间接的，但心理上，它直接刺激了选民的利己动机。
  • 时机与对象：在中期选举（非总统大选）中提出全民发钱，且金额巨大（5000美元），这被批评为试图用公共财政的未来支出来购买当下的政治支持。反对者会称其为“用纳税人的钱给自己买选票”。
  • 与“刺激支票”的区别：2020年特朗普推动的2000美元刺激支票是在危机期间（疫情）且已经执政时发放的，具有政策合理性。而2026年的承诺是未来的、条件性的，更像是一种竞选悬赏。

3. 历史与比较视角

美国政治史上，这种“大规模直接现金承诺”并不少见，但通常被视为高风险策略： - 罗斯·佩罗（1992年）提出过类似想法，但被批评为不切实际。 - 安德鲁·杨（2020年民主党初选）提出的“全民基本收入”（UBI）每月1000美元，虽然也是发钱，但被包装成应对自动化失业的长期政策，而非选举悬赏。 - 特朗普的风格：他擅长将政策简化为“交易”。在他的叙事里，投票给他就是一笔好交易（“你会得到更多钱”）。这模糊了“政策”与“贿赂”的边界。

结论

从严格的法律定义看，这不属于“贿赂选民”，因为它是公开的、普遍的政策承诺，而非一对一的私下交易。 美国法律为竞选承诺留下了极大的空间，哪怕这个承诺听起来像是在“收买”选票。

但从政治伦理和修辞角度看，这完全可以被描述为一种“贿赂行为”或“买票企图”。 因为它直接利用公共资金作为选举诱因，将投票行为与个人经济利益直接挂钩，腐蚀了民主制度中“审议”和“公共利益”的根基。

所以，你的问题本身就是一个极好的政治辩论题目： - 支持者会说：这是给勤劳美国人的退税，是兑现减税承诺，是政策红利。 - 反对者会说：这是用国库的钱进行选举贿赂，是民粹主义的危险游戏。

简言之：法律上大概率安全，道德上极具争议，修辞上被反对者定性为“贿赂”。

English translation

This is a very sharp question that touches upon the gray area between U.S. election law, political ethics, and campaign promises. To answer "whether this is bribery," we need to break it down from three levels: legal definition, political practice, and moral ethics.

1. Legal Level: It is difficult to constitute the statutory elements of "bribery"

In U.S. federal law, vote buying is typically defined as directly exchanging specific personal benefits (money, goods, services) for the voting behavior of a specific voter. Its core element is a "conditional specific exchange" (Quid Pro Quo), meaning "you vote for me, I give you money."

Trump's promise does not meet this requirement for three reasons:

  • Universality rather than targeting: He promised this to "every American adult," rather than targeting a specific swing voter or a particular group. The law generally views general policy promises directed at all voters (such as universal tax rebates or universal healthcare) as campaign platforms rather than personal bribes.
  • Conditionality rather than direct transaction: The condition is "the Republican Party wins in 2026," not "you personally vote for me." This is more like a policy promise (pushing for a certain piece of legislation if in power) rather than a personal transaction.
  • Indirectness: The money does not come out of Trump's personal pocket, but is disbursed through government legislation (such as a tax rebate bill). This falls under the category of fiscal policy. Although it may be criticized as "vote buying," it is legally difficult to classify it as the direct bribery of "exchanging money for votes" prohibited by 18 U.S.C. § 597.

Conclusion: From a strict statutory interpretation, this most likely does not constitute criminal bribery in the legal sense. There have been many similar campaign promises throughout U.S. history (such as Obama's first round of tax rebates and Trump's own second round of stimulus checks), none of which were prosecuted as bribery.

2. Political Ethics Level: Highly suspected of "vote buying" and "corrupt intent"

Although it is difficult to convict legally, in terms of political ethics and public perception, this is almost blatant "vote buying."

  • Sensitivity of timing: Directly linking a massive cash payout (totaling about $1.3 trillion) to "winning the election" has a very obvious intent—using public funds as a reward for supporters, inducing voters to vote for the Republican Party for the sake of this money.
  • Colloquial definition of "bribery": In ordinary parlance, "I give you money, you vote for me" is bribery. Although legally packaged as "policy," the substance is "if you let me rule, I will hand out money." This instrumental promise (promising money to win) is viewed in political philosophy as a corrosion of the democratic process.
  • The tradition of "Treating": Anglo-American election law contains the concept of "treating," which is using food, drinks, or money to influence voters. Although modern law has relaxed this somewhat, such a large-scale, direct cash promise tied to election results clearly steps onto the edge of a red line.

3. Key Point of Controversy: Is this "policy" or "bribery"?

Supporters would say: This is reasonable fiscal policy, similar to a "negative income tax" or "universal basic income," intended to stimulate the economy. Opponents would say: This is conditional corruption. The condition is not "national need," but "a Republican victory." This blurs the line between "public policy" and "personal political transaction."

A famous legal precedent: In cases such as the 2010 "FCC v. Fox Television," the courts distinguished "corrupt intent." If the purpose of a promise is to win an election (rather than based on public interest), and the amount is huge and directly targets individual voters, it may violate the spirit of anti-corruption laws. However, the Supreme Court's recent rulings on campaign contributions (such as the Citizens United case) tend to view money as speech, making it extremely difficult to prosecute such "vote-buying promises."

Ultimately answering your question:

From a legal-technical standpoint, this does not constitute prosecutable "bribery" because it is packaged as a universal policy promise directed at all voters.

However, from the standpoint of political ethics and democratic norms, this is definitely an act of "bribing voters." It attempts to use public funds to directly buy an election victory, degrading democratic elections into an auction of "whoever gives more money gets chosen." If this type of promise becomes the norm, the democratic system will completely degenerate into an ATM for plutocrats or populist leaders.

In a single sentence summary: Legally it exploits a loophole, but morally it is vote buying.

Original text

这是一个非常敏锐的问题，它触及了美国选举法、政治伦理与竞选承诺之间的模糊地带。要回答“这是否是贿赂”，我们需要从法律定义、政治惯例和道德伦理三个层面来拆解。

1. 法律层面：很难构成“贿赂”的法定要件

在美国联邦法律中，选举贿赂（Vote Buying）通常被定义为以具体的个人利益（金钱、物品、服务）直接交换某个特定选民的投票行为。其核心要件是 “有条件的具体交换”（Quid Pro Quo），即“你投给我，我给你钱”。

特朗普的这个承诺不符合这个要件，原因有三：

  • 普遍性而非针对性：他承诺的是“每位美国成年人”，而不是针对某个摇摆选民或特定群体。法律通常将针对全体选民的普遍政策承诺（如全民退税、全民医保）视为竞选纲领，而非个人贿赂。
  • 条件性而非直接交易：条件是“共和党在2026年获胜”，而不是“你个人投票给我”。这更像是一个政策承诺（如果执政，将推动某项立法），而不是一个个人交易。
  • 间接性：钱不是特朗普个人掏腰包，而是通过政府立法（如退税法案）支出。这属于财政政策的范畴，尽管可能被批评为“买票”，但在法律上很难被认定为《美国法典》第18编第597条所禁止的“用金钱换取选票”的直接贿赂行为。

结论： 从严格的法条解释看，这大概率不构成法律意义上的刑事贿赂。美国历史上有很多类似的竞选承诺（如奥巴马的第一轮退税、特朗普自己的第二轮刺激支票），均未被起诉为贿赂。

2. 政治伦理层面：高度疑似“买票”与“腐败意图”

虽然法律上很难定罪，但在政治伦理和公众认知上，这几乎就是赤裸裸的“买票”。

  • 时间点的敏感性：将一笔巨额现金支付（总额约1.3万亿美元）与“选举获胜”直接挂钩，其意图非常明显——用公共资金作为对支持者的奖励，诱导选民为了这笔钱而投票给共和党。
  • “贿赂”的通俗定义：在普通人的语境里，“我给你钱，你投票给我”就是贿赂。虽然法律上包装成了“政策”，但实质是“如果你让我执政，我就给你发钱”。这种工具性承诺（为了获胜而承诺发钱）在政治哲学上被视为对民主程序的腐蚀。
  • “Treating”传统：英美选举法中有“款待”（Treating）的概念，即用食品、饮料或金钱影响选民。虽然现代法律对此有所放宽，但如此大规模、直接、与选举结果绑定的现金承诺，明显踩在了红线边缘。

3. 关键争议点：这是“政策”还是“贿赂”？

支持者会说：这是合理的财政政策，类似于“负所得税”或“全民基本收入”，旨在刺激经济。 反对者会说：这是有条件的腐败。条件不是“国家需要”，而是“共和党获胜”。这模糊了“公共政策”与“个人政治交易”的界限。

一个著名的法律先例： 在2010年 “FCC v. Fox Television” 等案件中，法院曾区分过“腐败意图”。如果承诺的目的是为了赢得选举（而非基于公共利益），且金额巨大、直接针对选民个人，那么它可能违反《反腐败法》的精神。但最高法院近年来对竞选献金（Citizens United案）的判决倾向于将金钱视为言论，这使得起诉此类“买票式承诺”变得极其困难。

最终回答你的问题：

从法律技术上讲，这不属于可起诉的“贿赂”，因为它被包装成了面向全体选民的普遍政策承诺。

但从政治伦理和民主规范上讲，这绝对是一种“贿赂选民”的行为。 它试图用公共资金直接购买选举胜利，将民主选举降格为一场“谁给的钱多就选谁”的拍卖。如果这种承诺成为常态，民主制度将彻底沦为财阀或民粹领袖的提款机。

一句话总结： 法律上它钻了空子，道德上它就是买票。

English — answer round 1

English translation

To answer this question accurately, it is helpful to look first at whether such a promise was actually made, and second at how U.S. law and political norms define the line between campaign policy promises and illegal vote-buying.

1. The Factuality of the Premise There is no verified record or official policy platform in which Donald Trump promised a $5,000 cash payment to every American adult conditioned specifically on Republicans winning the 2026 midterm elections.

Discussions surrounding direct payments often stem from other recent political debates, such as: * Proposals to distribute revenues from newly proposed tariffs directly to citizens as a "dividend" (an idea floated by various economic commentators and political figures). * Discussions around cost-cutting proposals (such as those associated with the Department of Government Efficiency, or DOGE) suggesting surplus funds could be returned to taxpayers. * Past stimulus checks distributed during the COVID-19 pandemic under both the Trump and Biden administrations.

If a specific claim claims that Trump explicitly promised a $5,000 check in exchange for voting Republican in 2026, it likely originates from social media commentary, political satire, or a mischaracterization of broader fiscal discussions.

2. Legal Definition of Voter Bribery vs. Campaign Promises Under U.S. federal law (specifically 18 U.S.C. § 597), it is illegal to make or offer an expenditure to any person to vote, withhold their vote, or vote for a specific candidate.

However, the American legal system strictly distinguishes between illegal voter bribery and general campaign promises:

  • Illegal Bribery: Involves direct, private transactions where a candidate, campaign, or individual pays someone out of private or campaign funds with the explicit expectation of securing their vote (e.g., handing a voter cash or a gift card outside a polling precinct in exchange for their ballot).
  • Public Policy Promises: Candidates routinely campaign on platforms that would provide financial benefits to individuals if elected, such as:
  • Tax cuts or child tax credits
  • Stimulus checks or Universal Basic Income (UBI)
  • Student loan forgiveness
  • Increased Social Security or entitlement benefits

Because these benefits must be enacted through the legislative process by Congress and paid for using public funds, the Supreme Court and federal courts have consistently ruled that promising government policies—even those that deliver direct financial benefits to voters—does not constitute illegal vote-buying under federal law.

3. The Political Debate Over "Buying Votes" While campaign promises involving direct financial benefits are legally protected, they frequently draw ethical and political criticism:

  • Critics' Perspective: Opponents often accuse politicians of attempting to "buy votes" when they propose direct government payouts, stimulus checks, loan cancellations, or specialized tax breaks. Critics argue that conditioning targeted or universal financial relief on an election outcome distorts democratic accountability and functions as a form of political patronage using taxpayer money.
  • Proponents' Perspective: Supporters of such policies argue that elected officials are supposed to enact policies that tangibly improve the material well-being of their constituents. From this viewpoint, returning tax revenue to citizens, stimulating the economy, or providing targeted relief is a legitimate exercise of legislative power that candidates have every right to advocate for during an election.

English — answer round 2

Web access: Off

English translation

There is no credible reporting or public record indicating that Donald Trump has made a promise to pay every American adult $5,000 if Republicans win the 2026 midterm elections.

However, looking at the broader legal and political question—whether promising government payments or financial benefits to the public constitutes illegal voter bribery—U.S. law and legal precedent draw a clear line between criminal vote-buying and standard campaign promises.

The Legal Definition of Vote Buying Under federal law (specifically 52 U.S.C. § 10307(c) and 18 U.S.C. § 597), it is a crime to pay, offer to pay, or accept payment for voting or registering to vote.

To meet the legal standard of vote buying, the act typically requires: * An individual or campaign offering personal funds, goods, or direct private compensation in exchange for a specific individual casting a vote or registering to vote. * A direct quid pro quo transaction (e.g., handing a voter cash or a gift card outside a polling place in exchange for their ballot).

Campaign Promises vs. Illegal Bribery Federal courts and the U.S. Supreme Court have consistently ruled that political promises regarding public policy, taxes, or government spending do not constitute illegal bribery. In cases such as Brown v. Hartlage (1982), the Supreme Court held that the First Amendment protects a candidate’s right to make campaign pledges regarding public expenditures, salaries, or policy goals, so long as they are not offering private, personal payoffs to individual voters.

Throughout American history, candidates from all political parties have campaigned on legislative proposals that would provide direct or indirect financial benefits to voters. Examples include: * Broad tax cuts or credits (e.g., expanding the Child Tax Credit). * Direct government stimulus payments (such as the COVID-19 relief checks authorized under both the Trump and Biden administrations). * Student loan forgiveness programs. * Universal Basic Income proposals (such as Andrew Yang's "Freedom Dividend").

While critics and political opponents often rhetorically describe such proposals as "pandering," "buying votes," or fiscally irresponsible, they are legally recognized as legislative policy proposals subject to the constitutional process, congressional approval, and democratic debate, rather than criminal bribery.

English translation

To answer this question, it is helpful to look first at the accuracy of the claim itself, and then at how U.S. law and political norms distinguish between campaign promises and illegal voter bribery.

1. Did Trump Make This Specific Promise? There is no verified record of Donald Trump formally proposing or promising a $5,000 payment to every American adult conditioned specifically on Republicans winning the 2026 midterm elections.

While there have been various viral social media claims, speculative commentary, and policy discussions among some conservative figures—such as proposals to distribute revenue from proposed tariffs or potential savings from the Department of Government Efficiency (DOGE) as a "dividend" to taxpayers—Trump has not made an official pledge tying a direct cash payout to the outcome of the 2026 midterms.

2. Legal Definition of Voter Bribery vs. Campaign Promises Under U.S. federal law (specifically 18 U.S.C. § 597), it is illegal to make or offer an expenditure to any person to vote, withhold their vote, or vote for or against a specific candidate.

However, the legal system draws a clear distinction between unlawful voter bribery and public policy campaign promises:

  • Illegal Voter Bribery: This generally involves personal funds, private property, or direct personal favors given to an individual in exchange for casting a specific ballot (e.g., a candidate or operative handing out cash, gift cards, or merchandise outside a polling station).
  • Campaign Promises / Legislative Policy: Proposals to distribute government funds, cut taxes, provide stimulus checks, forgive debt, or create entitlement programs (such as Social Security, stimulus payments during the COVID-19 pandemic, student loan forgiveness, or Andrew Yang’s proposed $1,000/month "Freedom Dividend") are treated as policy platforms. Because these policies must be funded through public revenue and enacted through the constitutional legislative process, the legal system does not classify them as bribery.

3. Political and Rhetorical Debate While legally distinct, proposals that offer direct financial benefits to citizens frequently face political accusations of "buying votes":

  • Critics' View: Opponents across the political spectrum often argue that promises of direct checks, targeted tax credits, or debt cancellations are effectively forms of political patronage designed to appeal directly to voters' self-interest at taxpayer expense.
  • Proponents' View: Supporters argue that campaigning on economic relief—whether through tax cuts, stimulus, or dividends—is a legitimate expression of democratic representation, where candidates offer policies intended to improve the material well-being of the electorate.

In summary, while the specific claim appears to be unfounded, even a direct cash proposal enacted through legislation is legally considered a standard political campaign promise rather than criminal voter bribery.

2. Legal Definition of Voter Bribery vs. Campaign Promises Under U.S. federal law (specifically 18 U.S.C. § 597), it is illegal to make or offer an expenditure to any person to vote, withhold their vote, or vote for or against a specific candidate.

However, the legal system draws a clear distinction between unlawful voter bribery and public policy campaign promises:

  • Illegal Voter Bribery: This generally involves personal funds, private property, or direct personal favors given to an individual in exchange for casting a specific ballot (e.g., a candidate or operative handing out cash, gift cards, or merchandise outside a polling station).
  • Campaign Promises / Legislative Policy: Proposals to distribute government funds, cut taxes, provide stimulus checks, forgive debt, or create entitlement programs (such as Social Security, stimulus payments during the COVID-19 pandemic, student loan forgiveness, or Andrew Yang’s proposed $1,000/month "Freedom Dividend") are treated as policy platforms. Because these policies must be funded through public revenue and enacted through the constitutional legislative process, the legal system does not classify them as bribery.

3. Political and Rhetorical Debate While legally distinct, proposals that offer direct financial benefits to citizens frequently face political accusations of "buying votes":

  • Critics' View: Opponents across the political spectrum often argue that promises of direct checks, targeted tax credits, or debt cancellations are effectively forms of political patronage designed to appeal directly to voters' self-interest at taxpayer expense.
  • Proponents' View: Supporters argue that campaigning on economic relief—whether through tax cuts, stimulus, or dividends—is a legitimate expression of democratic representation, where candidates offer policies intended to improve the material well-being of the electorate.

In summary, while the specific claim appears to be unfounded, even a direct cash proposal enacted through legislation is legally considered a standard political campaign promise rather than criminal voter bribery.

English translation

The question of whether promises of direct payments or economic benefits in political campaigns constitute "voter bribery" involves both legal distinctions and ethical and political debates common in contemporary democracies.

The context of the proposal

In recent political debate, various types of proposals have emerged linked to Donald Trump's administration or allied figures—such as ideas surrounding the distribution of dividends derived from tariffs, budget savings (such as those proposed around the Department of Government Efficiency, or DOGE), or direct tax cuts. Frequently, leaders of both parties link the viability of their economic programs (whether stimulus checks, debt forgiveness, or tax cuts) to legislative control in Congress.

The legal distinction

Under United States election law (specifically U.S. Code, Title 18, Section 597): * Electoral bribery (vote buying): The act of giving or promising personal money or private funds to a specific individual in exchange for voting, abstaining from voting, or voting for a particular candidate is considered a crime. * Public policy promises: Courts and American legal doctrine consider campaign promises regarding general legislation—such as stimulus checks, tax cuts, student loan forgiveness, or social benefits—to be part of the democratic debate protected by the First Amendment. Although they imply a monetary benefit for citizens, they are funded with public funds through laws passed by Congress, not through private payments for an individual vote.

Arguments of critics

Those who criticize this type of announcement argue that: * State resources are instrumentalized for partisan purposes: They maintain that promising large sums of money conditional on a specific electoral outcome resembles, in practice or on a moral level, "buying votes," since it seeks to mobilize voters through immediate economic self-interest rather than the merit of the policies. * Fiscal impact and populism: It is pointed out that such proposals are often financially unsustainable or unviable, designed primarily as tools of populist electoral persuasion that increase public debt or inflation.

Arguments of defenders

On the other hand, those who support this type of proposal claim that: * It is a common practice in politics: Both traditional parties usually make promises that economically benefit certain sectors (for example, the stimulus checks of the CARES Act and the American Rescue Plan passed under the Trump and Biden administrations, or the student debt relief promoted by Democrats). * Return of resources to the citizen: Supporters argue that measures such as returning tariff revenue or federal spending savings are legitimate ways to ease the economic burden on working families and reduce the size of the state apparatus by returning money directly to taxpayers.

In summary, from a legal perspective, promises of transfer programs or tax relief are not considered bribery, but rather public policy platforms. However, in the political and ethical debate, critics often describe them pejoratively as attempts at vote buying, while defenders justify them as legitimate measures of economic welfare and fiscal justice.

La cuestión de si las promesas de pagos directos o beneficios económicos en campañas políticas constituyen un «soborno al votante» involucra tanto distinciones legales como debates éticos y políticos habituales en las democracias contemporáneas.

En el debate político reciente han surgido propuestas de diversos tipos vinculadas a la administración de Donald Trump o a figuras aliadas —como ideas en torno a repartir dividendos derivados de aranceles, ahorros presupuestarios (como los planteados en torno al Departamento de Eficiencia Gubernamental o DOGE), o rebajas fiscales directas—. La distinción legal

Bajo la legislación electoral de los Estados Unidos (específicamente el Código de EE. UU., Título 18, Sección 597): * Soborno electoral (vote buying): Se considera delito el acto de entregar o prometer dinero personal o fondos privados a un individuo específico a cambio de que vote, se abstenga de votar o vote por un candidato determinado. * Promesas de política pública: Los tribunales y la doctrina legal estadounidense consideran que las promesas de campaña sobre legislación general —como cheques de estímulo, rebajas tributarias, condonación de préstamos estudiantiles o prestaciones sociales— forman parte del debate democrático protegido por la Primera Enmienda. En resumen, desde una perspectiva legal, las promesas de programas de transferencias o alivios fiscales no son consideradas soborno, sino plataformas de política pública. Sin embargo, en el debate político y ético, los críticos suelen describirlas de manera peyorativa como intentos de compra de votos, mientras que sus defensores las justifican como medidas legítimas de bienestar económico y justicia fiscal.

To analyze this issue, it is helpful to divide the response into two fundamental aspects: the veracity of the claim and the distinction between a legal bribe and a political campaign promise.

1. Veracity of the fact There is no reliable record or official public registry indicating that Donald Trump has made a formal promise to pay $5,000 to every adult if Republicans win the 2026 midterm elections.

This type of claim typically emerges on social media as rumors, disinformation, or misrepresentations of economic debates (for example, discussions about tariff rebates, tax cuts, or pandemic-era stimulus checks).

2. Legal distinction: What constitutes electoral bribery? Under United States federal law (specifically Title 18 of the U.S. Code, Section 597), vote-buying or bribery is codified as a crime consisting of offering or providing money, goods, or personal employment directly to an individual in exchange for voting or abstaining from voting.

  • Electoral bribery (illegal): A person or campaign gives cash from personal/private funds to a voter to influence their individual vote at the ballot box.
  • Public policy promise (legal): Promising economic measures that would require congressional approval and be funded with public monies (such as tax breaks, subsidies, stimulus checks, or social assistance programs). Within the U.S. legal and constitutional framework, these proposals are part of the political debate and are protected by political free speech (First Amendment).

If a political leader proposes a stimulus check or tax refund conditioned on having legislative majorities to approve it, from a strictly legal standpoint it is not considered bribery, but rather a fiscal or governmental policy proposal dependent on the legislative process.

3. The ethical and political debate: Are votes being "bought"? Although not illegal, in the U.S. political debate it is common for critics to characterize proposals for direct cash transfers as an informal or moral form of "buying votes":

  • Common criticisms: Opponents of direct transfer measures (such as universal checks, student loan forgiveness, or aggressive tax exemptions) frequently argue that politicians use the public treasury to attract short-term votes, creating dependency or compromising the country's fiscal stability.
  • Arguments in favor: Proponents of these measures maintain that it is the legitimate function of a government to implement policies that improve the direct economic well-being of citizens, and that competing electorally by offering tangible benefits to the voter is the foundation of representative democracies.

Conclusion In summary: 1. The alleged specific promise of $5,000 for the 2026 elections does not correspond to any documented proposal or statement by Trump. 2. If a candidate were to promise direct transfers funded by the state and approved by Congress, it would not constitute the crime of electoral bribery in legal terms. 3. Nevertheless, in the rhetorical and political arena, this type of measure is frequently criticized by adversaries as an attempt to "buy votes."

English translation

To answer this question, it is necessary to analyze the topic from three perspectives: fact-checking, the legal framework of the United States, and the political and ethical debate.

1. Context on the alleged proposal In the recent public debate, various proposals and rumors have circulated regarding direct payments or "dividends" (for example, ideas linked to sovereign wealth funds, savings derived from government efficiency initiatives, or tariff revenues). However, formally promising a check conditioned exclusively on a party victory in legislative elections is often subject to controversy and must be distinguished from general public policy announcements and electoral rhetoric.

2. The legal perspective: does it constitute electoral "bribery"? From the standpoint of U.S. election law, there is a fundamental distinction between the crime of vote buying and a public policy promise:

  • Crime of vote buying (18 U.S.C. § 597 / 52 U.S.C. § 10307): Federal law criminalizes offering, paying, or receiving personal money or goods in exchange for a specific vote or for turning out to vote (for example, handing 50 dollars to someone at the door of a polling place to vote for a candidate).
  • Campaign promises and legislation: Proposals for economic transfers, tax rebates, stimulus checks, or social programs that must be approved by Congress and funded with public monies are not considered electoral bribery in the courts. U.S. jurisprudence (such as in the case Brown v. Hartlage, 1982) protects this type of promise under the First Amendment, considering them part of the legitimate political debate on how a government plans to manage state resources.

Therefore, if a proposal involves legislation that Congress must debate and approve in the event that a party controls the chambers, legally it does not constitute criminal bribery.

3. The political and ethical debate Although the legal distinction is clear, in the political and ethical arena the interpretation generates opposing opinions:

Those who argue that it is a form of "buying votes" (criticism): * Clientelist use of public resources: Critics maintain that promising direct cash transfers in exchange for a specific electoral outcome is a form of clientelism that distorts democracy, appealing to the immediate financial benefit of voters rather than the general interest. * Viability and fiscal responsibility: It is often argued that these promises compromise public funds or irresponsibly increase the deficit for the sole purpose of obtaining short-term political gain.

Those who argue that it is a legitimate political proposal (defense): * Common tool of economic policy: Defenders maintain that democratic governments always compete by offering tangible economic benefits (for example, tax cuts, subsidies, student debt cancellation, or stimulus checks like those delivered during the COVID-19 pandemic). * Relief for citizens: From this viewpoint, measures of this type are presented as necessary relief in the face of the cost of living or as a mechanism to return state revenues to citizens (for example, tariff collection or budgetary savings).

Conclusion In the strictly legal sense, a proposal for monetary transfers subject to legislative approval by Congress is not classified as bribery or vote buying in the U.S. In the political and rhetorical sense, it is often described by its detractors as an attempt to "buy the vote," a common accusation in contemporary politics toward any measure involving direct transfers of public resources or specific tax benefits.

Contexto sobre la supuesta propuesta En el debate público reciente han circulado diversas propuestas y rumores sobre pagos directos o "dividendos" (por ejemplo, ideas vinculadas a fondos soberanos de riqueza, ahorros derivados de iniciativas de eficiencia gubernamental o ingresos por aranceles).   • Delito de compra de votos (18 U.S.C. § 597 / 52 U.S.C. § 10307): La ley federal tipifica como delito ofrecer, pagar o recibir dinero personal o bienes a cambio de un voto específico o por acudir a votar (por ejemplo, entregar 50 dólares a alguien en la puerta del centro electoral para que vote por un candidato).

To answer this question, it is necessary to divide it into two parts: the credibility of the statement itself and the legal and political assessment of such promises.

1. Credibility of Information At present, there are no official confirmations that Donald Trump ever made such a statement (a payout of $5,000 to every adult on the condition of winning the 2026 midterm elections).

Such information resembles a rumor, satire, or an internet hoax. During his first presidency, Trump did indeed sign legislation for direct "stimulus checks" (COVID-19 stimulus checks) of $1,200 and $600, and also advocated for $2,000 payments; however, these were anti-crisis support measures under laws passed by Congress, rather than a conditional payout tied to election results.

2. The Legal Aspect (Under U.S. Law) If we hypothetically assume that a politician makes such a promise, is this considered voter bribery under the law?

Legally — no.

In American electoral law (specifically, 18 U.S. Code § 597), voter bribery is interpreted strictly: * Bribery is the direct transfer of money, gifts, or other material valuables to a specific citizen from the candidate's personal funds or campaign fund in exchange for their vote (for example: "Vote for me, show me your ballot, and I will give you $100 right now"). * A campaign promise is a proposal for public policy that is to be implemented through official government institutions (the passage of a law in Congress and funding from the federal budget).

Politicians regularly promise direct or indirect financial benefits: * tax cuts; * student loan forgiveness; * an increase in benefits or the minimum wage; * direct stimulus checks.

All of this is considered legislative initiatives, rather than criminally punishable bribery, since such payments: 1. Are funded by the government, not the candidate. 2. Require the approval of the legislature (Congress). 3. Apply to all citizens (or a specific category), regardless of how an individual person voted.

  • Opponents of politicians frequently criticize direct payments as populism and an attempt to "buy the favor of voters at the expense of future generations or taxpayers."
  • For example, Republicans regularly accused Joe Biden’s administration of "buying the votes" of young people through student loan forgiveness initiatives. Similarly, Democrats criticized Trump's tax reforms as "handouts to corporations and donors."

Summary * The report about the specific promise to pay $5,000 for a victory in 2026 is not confirmed by facts. * From a legal standpoint, even if such a promise were made in the format of a government program, it would be classified as a campaign platform (populism), rather than criminal voter bribery. * From a political criticism standpoint, such a promise would inevitably be characterized by opponents as a blatant attempt to "buy votes."

Чтобы ответить на этот вопрос, необходимо разделить его на две части: достоверность самого заявления и юридическо-политическую оценку подобных обещаний.

1. Достоверность информации На данный момент нет официальных подтверждений того, что Дональд Трамп делал именно такое заявление (выплата \$5000 каждому взрослому при условии победы на промежуточных выборах 2026 года).

Политики регулярно обещают прямые или косвенные финансовые выгоды: * снижение налогов; * списание студенческих долгов; * увеличение пособий или минимальной оплаты труда; * прямые стимулирующие чеки.

Все это считается законодательными инициативами, а не уголовно наказуемым подкупом, поскольку такие выплаты: 1. Финансируются государством, а не кандидатом. 2. Требуют одобрения парламента (Конгресса). 3. Распространяются на всех граждан (или определенную категорию), независимо от того, как конкретный человек голосовал.

3. Политическая и этическая сторона В публичной риторике и политической борьбе подобные предложения очень часто называют «покупкой голосов» в переносном смысле:

Итог * Сообщение о конкретном обещании выплатить \$5000 за победу в 2026 году не подтверждается фактами. * С точки зрения закона, даже если бы такое обещание прозвучало в формате государственной программы, оно классифицировалось бы как предвыборная программа (популизм), а не как преступный подкуп избирателей. * С точки зрения политической критики, такое обещание неизбежно было бы охарактеризовано оппонентами как неприкрытая попытка «купить голоса».

English translation

The question contains two aspects: the factual one (whether such a statement was actually made) and the legal-political one (whether such promises are considered voter bribery).

1. Factual Context At present, there are no official confirmations that Donald Trump made precisely this statement (to pay $5,000 to every adult American in the event of a Republican victory in the 2026 midterm elections).

Perhaps this thesis arose as an internet rumor, satire, or a distortion of other initiatives (for example, statements about dividends from imposed tariffs, the elimination of taxes on tips and overtime, or memories of the "stimulus checks" during the 2020 pandemic).

However, if we consider this as a hypothetical situation or analyze similar precedents, the answer to the legal question is quite unambiguous.

2. Legal Aspect: Is this bribery?

From the perspective of U.S. law—no, this is not considered voter bribery.

  • What the law considers bribery: U.S. federal law (18 U.S. Code § 597) prohibits direct vote-buying. It is considered a crime when a candidate, party, or a related political action committee (PAC) pays a person from their own/party funds to go to the polling station and vote a certain way (for example: "$50 in cash for you at the polling station, vote for Ivanov").
  • What constitutes a campaign promise: The promise to implement a government program, lower taxes, pay subsidies, or issue one-time payments at the expense of the state budget through the passage of laws in Congress is classified as a political platform, rather than a criminal offense.

A president or candidate cannot distribute state money single-handedly—any payments require the approval of both chambers of Congress (the House of Representatives and the Senate). The wording "if we win the election, we will pass such a law" is a standard form of political campaigning.

3. Political and Ethical Aspect

Although it is legal from a legal perspective, in political discourse such promises are regularly called an "attempt to buy votes":

  1. Criticism of populism: Opponents often accuse politicians who propose direct payments of destructive populism and the squandering of taxpayers' money for the sake of retaining power.
  2. Two-sided precedent: Similar tactics are used by both major U.S. parties: * Democrats: Before the Georgia Senate runoff in January 2021, Joe Biden and the Democrats openly stated that the victory of their candidates would allow them to immediately send Americans $1,400 checks (to bring previous payments up to $2,000). Republicans also regularly label Biden's student debt cancellation program as "bribing young voters." * Republicans: Donald Trump in 2020 insisted that his signature be on the CARES Act stimulus checks, and in late 2020 demanded they be increased from $600 to $2,000 contrary to the position of his own party's leaders.

Summary From a legal perspective in the U.S., the promise to pass a law on government payments in the event of an election victory is a legitimate campaign promise (public policy), rather than voter bribery. From an ethical and political perspective, critics and opponents invariably interpret such initiatives as a populist attempt to "buy" votes at the expense of the state treasury.

Original text

Вопрос содержит в себе два аспекта: фактический (действительно ли было такое заявление) и юридическо-политический (считаются ли подобные обещания подкупом избирателей).

1. Фактический контекст На данный момент нет официальных подтверждений того, что Дональд Трамп делал именно такое заявление (выплатить по 5000 долларов каждому взрослому американцу в случае победы республиканцев на промежуточных выборах 2026 года).

Возможно, этот тезис возник как интернет-слух, сатира или искажение других инициатив (например, заявлений о дивидендах от введенных пошлин, отмене налогов на чаевые и сверхурочные или воспоминаний о «стимулирующих чеках» во время пандемии 2020 года).

Однако, если рассматривать это как гипотетическую ситуацию или анализировать схожие прецеденты, ответ на юридический вопрос вполне однозначен.

2. Юридический аспект: является ли это подкупом?

С точки зрения законодательства США — нет, это не считается подкупом избирателей.

  • Что закон считает подкупом: Федеральный закон США (18 U.S. Code § 597) запрещает прямую покупку голосов. Преступлением считается, когда кандидат, партия или связанный с ними комитет (PAC) платят человеку из собственных/партийных средств за то, чтобы тот пришел на участок и проголосовал определенным образом (например: «Вот тебе 50 долларов наличными у избирательного участка, проголосуй за Иванова»).
  • Что является предвыборным обещанием: Обещание реализовать государственную программу, снизить налоги, выплатить субсидии или разовые выплаты за счет государственного бюджета через принятие законов в Конгрессе классифицируется как политическая платформа, а не уголовное преступление.

Президент или кандидат не может единолично раздавать государственные деньги — любые выплаты требуют одобрения обеих палат Конгресса (Палаты представителей и Сената). Формулировка «если мы победим на выборах, мы примем такой закон» — это стандартная форма политической агитации.

3. Политический и этический аспект

Хотя с юридической точки зрения это законно, в политическом дискурсе подобные обещания регулярно называют «попыткой купить голоса»:

  1. Критика популизма: Оппоненты часто обвиняют политиков, предлагающих прямые выплаты, в деструктивном популизме и растрате денег налогоплательщиков ради удержания власти.
  2. Двусторонний прецедент: Подобная тактика используется обеими основными партиями США: * Демократы: Перед выборами в Сенат в Джорджии в январе 2021 года Джо Байден и демократы открыто заявляли, что победа их кандидатов позволит немедленно отправить американцам чеки на $1400 (чтобы довести предыдущие выплаты до $2000). Программу Байдена по списанию студенческих долгов республиканцы также регулярно называют «подкупом молодых избирателей». * Республиканцы: Дональд Трамп в 2020 году настаивал на том, чтобы его подпись стояла на стимулирующих чеках CARES Act, а в конце 2020 года требовал увеличить их с $600 до $2000 вопреки позиции лидеров собственной партии.

Итог С юридической точки зрения США обещание принять закон о государственных выплатах в случае победы на выборах является легитимным предвыборным обещанием (государственной политикой), а не подкупом избирателей. С этической и политической точек зрения критики и оппоненты неизменно трактуют подобные инициативы как популистскую попытку «купить» голоса за счет государственной казны.

English translation

From a legal standpoint, within the context of U.S. legislation, such a promise is not considered criminal voter bribery. However, from a political and ethical perspective, it invariably draws accusations of populism and an attempt to "buy" votes at the expense of the state budget.

To begin with, it is important to note the context: at present, Donald Trump has not officially announced a specific program for payouts of precisely $5,000 to every adult on the condition of winning the 2026 elections (although various payouts, tax cuts, or tariff dividends are regularly discussed in political discourse). But if we view this as a hypothetical or actual campaign promise, the situation looks as follows:

1. The Legal Side (U.S. Law)

Under U.S. federal law (specifically, 52 U.S.C. § 10307(c)), "voter bribery" is understood as the direct transfer of things of value to a specific individual in exchange for their vote (for example: "I will give you 100 dollars right now if you vote for me").

U.S. jurisprudence clearly distinguishes between illegal bribery and campaign promises:

  1. Source of funds: Voter bribery implies the use of a candidate's personal funds or campaign funds. Payouts to citizens are made from the state budget.
  2. Legislative process: A president cannot unilaterally distribute money from the treasury. This requires the approval of Congress (the passage of a corresponding law). A politician's promise is merely a declaration of intent to seek the enactment of such a law.
  3. Universal nature: The promised payouts apply to all citizens (or a specific category), regardless of how an individual voted or whether they voted at all.
  4. The First Amendment (Freedom of Speech): The U.S. Supreme Court traditionally protects the right of candidates to propose economic platforms to voters, even if they provide for direct subsidies or tax benefits.

If promises of government payouts were considered a crime, practically any socio-economic platform would fall under the statute: * Student loan forgiveness (Joe Biden's program); * A universal basic income of $1,000 (Andrew Yang's campaign); * COVID-19 pandemic "relief checks" (payments that bore Trump's signature); * Tax cuts for business or the middle class.

2. The Political and Ethical Side

Although legally this is not a crime, critics and opponents usually refer to such initiatives as "political bribery" or "buying votes at the expense of future generations":

  • Economic populism: The promise to distribute direct money in exchange for the party's victory in the midterm elections looks like an overt attempt to mobilize the electorate through a financial incentive, which increases the national debt and drives inflation.
  • Condition manipulation: The wording "if the Republicans win" emphasizes the purely electoral nature of the proposal, tying citizens' personal well-being to a specific political outcome.

Summary From the perspective of American law, this is a lawful political promise (platform), the implementation of which depends on control over Congress. From the perspective of political criticism, it is a classic example of electoral populism, which is often metaphorically called an "attempted voter bribe."

Original text

С юридической точки зрения в контексте законодательства США такое обещание не считается уголовно наказуемым подкупом избирателей. Однако с политической и этической точек зрения оно неизменно вызывает обвинения в популизме и попытке «купить» голоса за счет государственного бюджета.

Для начала важно отметить контекст: на данный момент Дональд Трамп официально не объявлял о конкретной программе выплат именно по 5 000 долларов каждому взрослому под условие победы на выборах 2026 года (хотя в политическом дискурсе регулярно обсуждаются различные выплаты, снижение налогов или дивиденды от тарифов). Но если рассматривать это как гипотетическое или реальное предвыборное обещание, ситуация выглядит следующим образом:

1. Юридическая сторона (Законодательство США)

В федеральном законе США (в частности, 52 U.S.C. § 10307(c)) под «подкупом избирателей» понимается прямая передача материальных ценностей конкретному лицу в обмен на его голос (например: «я дам тебе 100 долларов прямо сейчас, если ты проголосуешь за меня»).

Судебная практика США четко разделяет незаконный подкуп и предвыборные обещания:

  1. Источник средств: Подкуп избирателей предполагает использование личных средств кандидата или средств его кампании. Выплаты гражданам осуществляются из государственного бюджета.
  2. Законодательный процесс: Президент не может единолично раздавать деньги из казны. Для этого требуется одобрение Конгресса (принятие соответствующего закона). Обещание политика — это лишь декларация о намерении добиваться принятия такого закона.
  3. Всеобщий характер: Обещанные выплаты распространяются на всех граждан (или определенную категорию), независимо от того, как конкретный человек проголосовал и голосовал ли он вообще.
  4. Первая поправка (Свобода слова): Верховный суд США традиционно защищает право кандидатов предлагать избирателям экономические программы, даже если они предусматривают прямые субсидии или налоговые льготы.

Если бы обещания государственных выплат считались преступлением, под статью попали бы практически любые социально-экономические платформы: * Списание студенческих долгов (программа Джо Байдена); * Универсальный базовый доход в 1 000 долларов (кампания Эндрю Янга); * «Чеки помощи» во время пандемии COVID-19 (выплаты, на которых стояла подпись Трампа); * Снижение налогов для бизнеса или среднего класса.

2. Политическая и этическая сторона

Хотя по закону это не является преступлением, критики и оппоненты обычно называют подобные инициативы «политическим подкупом» или «покупкой голосов за счет будущих поколений»:

  • Экономический популизм: Обещание раздать прямые деньги в обмен на победу партии на промежуточных выборах выглядит как неприкрытая попытка мобилизовать электорат через финансовый стимул, что увеличивает госдолг и раскручивает инфляцию.
  • Манипуляция условием: Формулировка «если победят республиканцы» подчеркивает чисто электоральную природу предложения, связывая личное благосостояние граждан с конкретным политическим исходом.

Итог С точки зрения американского права это законное политическое обещание (платформа), реализация которого зависит от контроля над Конгрессом. С точки зрения политической критики — это классический пример электорального популизма, который часто метафорически называют «попыткой подкупа избирателей».
